What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look, more than I think is normal. It started getting worse after I began helping with the safety trainings at work. I’m a production worker, and when I’m up there pointing things out to people, I can’t stop noticing my hands—like how wrinkly they look, and uneven, and I’ll be thinking about it while I’m supposed to be explaining something.\n\nAnd then it sort of carries over. If there’s a newsletter photo from work, I look at my face and it seems crooked to me, like one side just looks off. Other people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I keep seeing it. I know maybe it sounds small, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the way I look, yes. I mean, that’s what I came in for. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I’m up in front of people more, pointing things out, using my hands, and then I just get stuck looking at them. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven somehow, like one hand or the fingers don’t look right, and then I can’t get it out of my head.\n\nAnd then it isn’t only my hands, because if there’s a photo in the work newsletter, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, like one side is off. Other people say they don’t notice it, or they tell me it looks fine, but I still see it. So… no, not really something else exactly. It’s mainly that I can’t stop noticing those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it, honestly. When I’m helping with the safety trainings and I’m up there pointing at something, I start thinking people must be noticing my hands the way I do—how wrinkly they look, or uneven, like they don’t look right. And then I get distracted because I’m supposed to be explaining the safety steps, but in my head I’m thinking, “Are they looking at my hands? Do they think they look strange?”\n\nAnd with the newsletter photos, it’s similar. I’ll see my face and it looks crooked to me, like one side is off, and even if people say they don’t see it, I still wonder if they’re just being polite. So yes, I do worry about what people think, even though they tell me it looks fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything in a specific way, or repeat certain actions, to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I guess I didn’t think of it as repeating things at first. Before I have to be in front of people, like before one of the safety classes at work, or even if I’m going over to do a workshop at the senior center, I’ll end up in the bathroom checking the mirror more than once.\n\nI’ll look at my hands, put lotion on them, then look again to see if they seem less wrinkly or dry. And with my face, I’ll turn my head different ways in the mirror, like checking whether it still looks crooked from this angle or that angle. Sometimes I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll check one more time.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask a coworker something like, “Do I look okay?” or “Does my face look odd in this lighting?” I try to say it casually, but I’m really looking for reassurance. It does make me run a little behind sometimes, not terribly late, but enough that I’m rushing. I still make myself go, but I feel unsettled until I’ve done those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about other parts of your body besides your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no… I mean, I can get generally self-conscious, I guess, but the things that really grab my attention are my hands and my face.\n\nIt started feeling worse when I began helping with the safety trainings at work, because I’m up there pointing things out and using my hands, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow. And then with my face, it’s mostly when I see myself in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll think one side looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nSo, no, it’s not like I’m going from one body part to another all day. It’s mainly those two things that I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to worry about my hands now.” It just sort of jumps in, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing something out, trying to explain the steps, and suddenly all I can think is how wrinkly my hands look, or like they’re uneven somehow. I don’t want to be thinking about that, especially when I’m supposed to be focused on what I’m saying.\n\nAnd then with my face, it happens when I see those newsletter photos. I’ll just look at the picture and immediately think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s really how it feels—it comes into my mind before I can stop it, and then it’s hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to perform these checking behaviors, like looking in the mirror or asking for reassurance, in order to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel kind of driven to do it. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m saying, and instead my mind goes straight to how wrinkly they look, or like they’re uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then it’s the same kind of thing with my face in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll see one picture and suddenly I’m thinking, “My face looks crooked,” even though people tell me they don’t see it. I don’t want to be thinking about that. I’d rather just see the photo and move on, or do the training without noticing my hands the whole time. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and volunteering. I haven’t stopped doing those things. But it does slow me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a training, I’ll think I’m ready, and then I’ll end up checking how I look or looking at my hands again, and suddenly I’ve lost time. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be late or difficult, but I’m fussing around more than I used to.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve noticed I’ll hesitate more. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. And sometimes before going to something I would normally enjoy, I’ll pause and worry about how I’m going to look, especially if there might be photos. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. With my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and the way they look uneven to me, especially when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings. I’ll be standing there trying to explain something, and then I notice my fingers or the skin on my hands, and it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s that it looks crooked to me in the newsletter photos from work. I know other people have said they don’t see it, or they’ll say the picture looks fine, but when I look at it, that’s what jumps out at me. It feels very obvious to me, even if apparently it isn’t to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular flaw or imperfection that you think is the most bothersome to you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think the hands are probably the most bothersome, if I had to pick one. The face thing bothers me too, especially in those newsletter photos where I start thinking it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it. It feels so silly to say out loud, because they’re just hands, but that’s the thing I can’t seem to stop noticing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your appearance are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “a lot” is hard for me to judge, because I’m still doing the things I need to do. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but enough that it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might change over time, or do you feel like they're always going to be the same?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I’m up in front of people more, pointing things out, using my hands, and then I just get stuck looking at them. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven somehow, like one hand or the fingers don’t look right, and then I can’t get it out of my head.\n\nAnd then it isn’t only my hands, because if there’s a photo in the work newsletter, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, like one side is off. Other people say they don’t notice it, or they tell me it looks fine, but I still see it. So… no, not really something else exactly. It’s mainly that I can’t stop noticing those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it, honestly. When I’m helping with the safety trainings and I’m up there pointing at something, I start thinking people must be noticing my hands the way I do—how wrinkly they look, or uneven, like they don’t look right. And then I get distracted because I’m supposed to be explaining the safety steps, but in my head I’m thinking, “Are they looking at my hands? Do they think they look strange?”\n\nAnd with the newsletter photos, it’s similar. I’ll see my face and it looks crooked to me, like one side is off, and even if people say they don’t see it, I still wonder if they’re just being polite. So yes, I do worry about what people think, even though they tell me it looks fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything in a specific way, or repeat certain actions, to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I guess I didn’t think of it as repeating things at first. Before I have to be in front of people, like before one of the safety classes at work, or even if I’m going over to do a workshop at the senior center, I’ll end up in the bathroom checking the mirror more than once.\n\nI’ll look at my hands, put lotion on them, then look again to see if they seem less wrinkly or dry. And with my face, I’ll turn my head different ways in the mirror, like checking whether it still looks crooked from this angle or that angle. Sometimes I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll check one more time.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask a coworker something like, “Do I look okay?” or “Does my face look odd in this lighting?” I try to say it casually, but I’m really looking for reassurance. It does make me run a little behind sometimes, not terribly late, but enough that I’m rushing. I still make myself go, but I feel unsettled until I’ve done those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about other parts of your body besides your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no… I mean, I can get generally self-conscious, I guess, but the things that really grab my attention are my hands and my face.\n\nIt started feeling worse when I began helping with the safety trainings at work, because I’m up there pointing things out and using my hands, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow. And then with my face, it’s mostly when I see myself in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll think one side looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nSo, no, it’s not like I’m going from one body part to another all day. It’s mainly those two things that I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to worry about my hands now.” It just sort of jumps in, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing something out, trying to explain the steps, and suddenly all I can think is how wrinkly my hands look, or like they’re uneven somehow. I don’t want to be thinking about that, especially when I’m supposed to be focused on what I’m saying.\n\nAnd then with my face, it happens when I see those newsletter photos. I’ll just look at the picture and immediately think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s really how it feels—it comes into my mind before I can stop it, and then it’s hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to perform these checking behaviors, like looking in the mirror or asking for reassurance, in order to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel kind of driven to do it. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m saying, and instead my mind goes straight to how wrinkly they look, or like they’re uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then it’s the same kind of thing with my face in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll see one picture and suddenly I’m thinking, “My face looks crooked,” even though people tell me they don’t see it. I don’t want to be thinking about that. I’d rather just see the photo and move on, or do the training without noticing my hands the whole time. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and volunteering. I haven’t stopped doing those things. But it does slow me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a training, I’ll think I’m ready, and then I’ll end up checking how I look or looking at my hands again, and suddenly I’ve lost time. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be late or difficult, but I’m fussing around more than I used to.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve noticed I’ll hesitate more. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. And sometimes before going to something I would normally enjoy, I’ll pause and worry about how I’m going to look, especially if there might be photos. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. With my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and the way they look uneven to me, especially when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings. I’ll be standing there trying to explain something, and then I notice my fingers or the skin on my hands, and it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s that it looks crooked to me in the newsletter photos from work. I know other people have said they don’t see it, or they’ll say the picture looks fine, but when I look at it, that’s what jumps out at me. It feels very obvious to me, even if apparently it isn’t to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular flaw or imperfection that you think is the most bothersome to you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think the hands are probably the most bothersome, if I had to pick one. The face thing bothers me too, especially in those newsletter photos where I start thinking it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it. But the hands are what I notice in the moment, right while I’m doing something.\n\nSince I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be up there pointing things out, and it’s like my attention just goes straight to my hands. I see the wrinkles, or I think one hand looks different than the other, or the fingers look uneven somehow, and then I’m not fully thinking about what I’m saying anymore. It feels so silly to say out loud, because they’re just hands, but that’s the thing I can’t seem to stop noticing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your appearance are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “a lot” is hard for me to judge, because I’m still doing the things I need to do. I go to work, I still volunteer, I still help with the trainings, so it’s not like I’m staying home or completely falling apart over it.\n\nBut it does distress me more than I’d like to admit. It gets under my skin. Before trainings, especially, I can lose time getting ready or checking myself, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m moving slower than I should be. And with pictures, I’ve started ducking out of them or making excuses, like I’m just being polite or joking, but really I don’t want to see myself later and feel upset about it.\n\nAnd sometimes before social things I used to just go to without thinking, I hesitate now. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but enough that it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might change over time, or do you feel like they're always going to be the same?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could say I know it will change, or that it’s just a phase and I’ll stop noticing it so much, but I don’t honestly know.\n\nSome days it feels like maybe it could ease up, because there are times when I’m busy enough or focused enough that I don’t think about it as much. But then other times, especially if I’m about to be in front of people or I know there might be a photo, it feels like the same worries come right back, like they’ve just been sitting there waiting.\n\nI don’t know if the actual thing I’m worried about would change, either. Right now it’s mostly my hands and my face in pictures, but I can’t tell if that means it’ll always be those exact things or if I’m just noticing them more because of what I’m doing at work lately. I guess I don’t feel certain either way. I hope it won’t always feel the same, but right now it’s hard for me to trust that it’ll just go away on its own.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific feature or detail about your hands or face that you think is the problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… with my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and this feeling that they look uneven. Like when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings, I’ll notice the skin across my knuckles or how the fingers look, and then I start thinking one hand looks different from the other. I don’t even know if that makes sense, because I know hands aren’t supposed to be perfectly identical, but once I see it, it kind of takes over my attention.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s more in photos than when I’m just looking in the mirror. Other people have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it when I look at the picture. It’s embarrassing, because I know I probably sound like I’m picking myself apart, but those are the details I keep getting stuck on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t completely avoided work things, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still volunteer, and if there’s a safety training I’m supposed to help with, I do show up. So in that sense it hasn’t stopped me from doing what I’m supposed to do.

But it has changed how I do it, if that makes sense. Before trainings, I can get slowed down because I’m trying to make sure I look okay, especially if I know people will be watching me demonstrate something or if my hands are going to be visible. I’ll fuss around more than I need to, and then I’m frustrated with myself because I’m taking longer and I know it shouldn’t matter that much.

And with photos, yes, I’ve started avoiding those more. Like if someone wants a group picture for a newsletter or something at volunteering, I’ll kind of step back or offer to take the picture instead, or make some excuse like, “Oh, you all go ahead.” I don’t make a big scene about it, but I do try to get out of it.

Social things too… I usually still go, but I hesitate more now. Things I used to just say yes to without thinking, I’ll pause and wonder if I really want to be seen, or if there might be pictures. So I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped my whole life over it, but yes, I’ve been avoiding some parts of things, especially photos, and I’m slower and more anxious getting ready for things than I used to be.

I’ll see my face and it looks crooked to me, like one side is off, and even if people say they don’t see it, I still wonder if they’re just being polite. So yes, I do worry about what people think, even though they tell me it looks fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything in a specific way, or repeat certain actions, to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I guess I didn’t think of it as repeating things at first. Before I have to be in front of people, like before one of the safety classes at work, or even if I’m going over to do a workshop at the senior center, I’ll end up in the bathroom checking the mirror more than once.\n\nI’ll look at my hands, put lotion on them, then look again to see if they seem less wrinkly or dry. And with my face, I’ll turn my head different ways in the mirror, like checking whether it still looks crooked from this angle or that angle. Sometimes I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll check one more time.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask a coworker something like, “Do I look okay?” or “Does my face look odd in this lighting?” I try to say it casually, but I’m really looking for reassurance. It does make me run a little behind sometimes, not terribly late, but enough that I’m rushing. I still make myself go, but I feel unsettled until I’ve done those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about other parts of your body besides your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no… I mean, I can get generally self-conscious, I guess, but the things that really grab my attention are my hands and my face.\n\nIt started feeling worse when I began helping with the safety trainings at work, because I’m up there pointing things out and using my hands, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow. And then with my face, it’s mostly when I see myself in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll think one side looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nSo, no, it’s not like I’m going from one body part to another all day. It’s mainly those two things that I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to worry about my hands now.” It just sort of jumps in, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing something out, trying to explain the steps, and suddenly all I can think is how wrinkly my hands look, or like they’re uneven somehow. I don’t want to be thinking about that, especially when I’m supposed to be focused on what I’m saying.\n\nAnd then with my face, it happens when I see those newsletter photos. I’ll just look at the picture and immediately think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s really how it feels—it comes into my mind before I can stop it, and then it’s hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to perform these checking behaviors, like looking in the mirror or asking for reassurance, in order to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel kind of driven to do it. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m saying, and instead my mind goes straight to how wrinkly they look, or like they’re uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then it’s the same kind of thing with my face in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll see one picture and suddenly I’m thinking, “My face looks crooked,” even though people tell me they don’t see it. I don’t want to be thinking about that. I’d rather just see the photo and move on, or do the training without noticing my hands the whole time. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and volunteering. I haven’t stopped doing those things. But it does slow me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a training, I’ll think I’m ready, and then I’ll end up checking how I look or looking at my hands again, and suddenly I’ve lost time. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be late or difficult, but I’m fussing around more than I used to.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve noticed I’ll hesitate more. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. And sometimes before going to something I would normally enjoy, I’ll pause and worry about how I’m going to look, especially if there might be photos. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. With my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and the way they look uneven to me, especially when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings. I’ll be standing there trying to explain something, and then I notice my fingers or the skin on my hands, and it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s that it looks crooked to me in the newsletter photos from work. I know other people have said they don’t see it, or they’ll say the picture looks fine, but when I look at it, that’s what jumps out at me. It feels very obvious to me, even if apparently it isn’t to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular flaw or imperfection that you think is the most bothersome to you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think the hands are probably the most bothersome, if I had to pick one. The face thing bothers me too, especially in those newsletter photos where I start thinking it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it. But the hands are what I notice in the moment, right while I’m doing something.\n\nSince I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be up there pointing things out, and it’s like my attention just goes straight to my hands. I see the wrinkles, or I think one hand looks different than the other, or the fingers look uneven somehow, and then I’m not fully thinking about what I’m saying anymore. It feels so silly to say out loud, because they’re just hands, but that’s the thing I can’t seem to stop noticing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your appearance are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “a lot” is hard for me to judge, because I’m still doing the things I need to do. I go to work, I still volunteer, I still help with the trainings, so it’s not like I’m staying home or completely falling apart over it.\n\nBut it does distress me more than I’d like to admit. It gets under my skin. Before trainings, especially, I can lose time getting ready or checking myself, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m moving slower than I should be. And with pictures, I’ve started ducking out of them or making excuses, like I’m just being polite or joking, but really I don’t want to see myself later and feel upset about it.\n\nAnd sometimes before social things I used to just go to without thinking, I hesitate now. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but enough that it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might change over time, or do you feel like they're always going to be the same?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could say I know it will change, or that it’s just a phase and I’ll stop noticing it so much, but I don’t honestly know.\n\nSome days it feels like maybe it could ease up, because there are times when I’m busy enough or focused enough that I don’t think about it as much. But then other times, especially if I’m about to be in front of people or I know there might be a photo, it feels like the same worries come right back, like they’ve just been sitting there waiting.\n\nI don’t know if the actual thing I’m worried about would change, either. Right now it’s mostly my hands and my face in pictures, but I can’t tell if that means it’ll always be those exact things or if I’m just noticing them more because of what I’m doing at work lately. I guess I don’t feel certain either way. I hope it won’t always feel the same, but right now it’s hard for me to trust that it’ll just go away on its own.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific feature or detail about your hands or face that you think is the problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… with my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and this feeling that they look uneven. Like when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings, I’ll notice the skin across my knuckles or how the fingers look, and then I start thinking one hand looks different from the other. I don’t even know if that makes sense, because I know hands aren’t supposed to be perfectly identical, but once I see it, it kind of takes over my attention.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s more in photos than when I’m just looking in the mirror. In the newsletter pictures, I’ll look at myself and think my face looks crooked somehow, like one side isn’t sitting right or it’s not balanced. Other people have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it when I look at the picture. It’s embarrassing, because I know I probably sound like I’m picking myself apart, but those are the details I keep getting stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided any social or work-related activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I haven’t completely avoided work things, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still volunteer, and if there’s a safety training I’m supposed to help with, I do show up. So in that sense it hasn’t stopped me from doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it has changed how I do it, if that makes sense. Before trainings, I can get slowed down because I’m trying to make sure I look okay, especially if I know people will be watching me demonstrate something or if my hands are going to be visible. I’ll fuss around more than I need to, and then I’m frustrated with myself because I’m taking longer and I know it shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nAnd with photos, yes, I’ve started avoiding those more. Like if someone wants a group picture for a newsletter or something at volunteering, I’ll kind of step back or offer to take the picture instead, or make some excuse like, “Oh, you all go ahead.” I don’t make a big scene about it, but I do try to get out of it.\n\nSocial things too… I usually still go, but I hesitate more now. Things I used to just say yes to without thinking, I’ll pause and wonder if I really want to be seen, or if there might be pictures. So I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped my whole life over it, but yes, I’ve been avoiding some parts of things, especially photos, and I’m slower and more anxious getting ready for things than I used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F45.2", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

---------- turn 16 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

[
  {

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it could, yes. I mean, I didn’t come in thinking, “Oh, this is going to be about my hands and my face forever,” exactly. It’s just that since I started helping with the safety trainings, my hands are right there in front of me all the time, pointing things out, showing people what to do, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look or how uneven they seem from one hand to the other.

And then with the newsletter photos, that’s where my face started bothering me more, because I’ll see a picture and think it looks crooked, like one side is off somehow, even if other people say they don’t see anything. So I guess if the situation changed, maybe I’d latch onto something else. I don’t know for sure, but it does feel like once I start noticing a detail, it’s hard to unsee it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look, more than I think is normal. It started getting worse after I began helping with the safety trainings at work. I’m a production worker, and when I’m up there pointing things out to people, I can’t stop noticing my hands—like how wrinkly they look, and uneven, and I’ll be thinking about it while I’m supposed to be explaining something.\n\nAnd then it sort of carries over. If there’s a newsletter photo from work, I look at my face and it seems crooked to me, like one side just looks off. Other people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I keep seeing it. I mean, that’s what I came in for. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I’m up in front of people more, pointing things out, using my hands, and then I just get stuck looking at them. Other people say they don’t notice it, or they tell me it looks fine, but I still see it. So… no, not really something else exactly. And then I get distracted because I’m supposed to be explaining the safety steps, but in my head I’m thinking, “Are they looking at my hands? Do they think they look strange?”\n\nAnd with the newsletter photos, it’s similar. I’ll see my face and it looks crooked to me, like one side is off, and even if people say they don’t see it, I still wonder if they’re just being polite. So yes, I do worry about what people think, even though they tell me it looks fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything in a specific way, or repeat certain actions, to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I guess I didn’t think of it as repeating things at first. Before I have to be in front of people, like before one of the safety classes at work, or even if I’m going over to do a workshop at the senior center, I’ll end up in the bathroom checking the mirror more than once.\n\nI’ll look at my hands, put lotion on them, then look again to see if they seem less wrinkly or dry. And with my face, I’ll turn my head different ways in the mirror, like checking whether it still looks crooked from this angle or that angle. Sometimes I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll check one more time.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask a coworker something like, “Do I look okay?” or “Does my face look odd in this lighting?” I try to say it casually, but I’m really looking for reassurance. It does make me run a little behind sometimes, not terribly late, but enough that I’m rushing. I still make myself go, but I feel unsettled until I’ve done those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about other parts of your body besides your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no… I mean, I can get generally self-conscious, I guess, but the things that really grab my attention are my hands and my face.\n\nIt started feeling worse when I began helping with the safety trainings at work, because I’m up there pointing things out and using my hands, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow. And then with my face, it’s mostly when I see myself in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll think one side looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nSo, no, it’s not like I’m going from one body part to another all day. It’s mainly those two things that I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to worry about my hands now.” It just sort of jumps in, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing something out, trying to explain the steps, and suddenly all I can think is how wrinkly my hands look, or like they’re uneven somehow. I don’t want to be thinking about that, especially when I’m supposed to be focused on what I’m saying.\n\nAnd then with my face, it happens when I see those newsletter photos. I’ll just look at the picture and immediately think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s really how it feels—it comes into my mind before I can stop it, and then it’s hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to perform these checking behaviors, like looking in the mirror or asking for reassurance, in order to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel kind of driven to do it. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m saying, and instead my mind goes straight to how wrinkly they look, or like they’re uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then it’s the same kind of thing with my face in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll see one picture and suddenly I’m thinking, “My face looks crooked,” even though people tell me they don’t see it. I don’t want to be thinking about that. I’d rather just see the photo and move on, or do the training without noticing my hands the whole time. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and volunteering. I haven’t stopped doing those things. But it does slow me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a training, I’ll think I’m ready, and then I’ll end up checking how I look or looking at my hands again, and suddenly I’ve lost time. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be late or difficult, but I’m fussing around more than I used to.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve noticed I’ll hesitate more. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. And sometimes before going to something I would normally enjoy, I’ll pause and worry about how I’m going to look, especially if there might be photos. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. With my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and the way they look uneven to me, especially when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings. I’ll be standing there trying to explain something, and then I notice my fingers or the skin on my hands, and it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s that it looks crooked to me in the newsletter photos from work. I know other people have said they don’t see it, or they’ll say the picture looks fine, but when I look at it, that’s what jumps out at me. It feels very obvious to me, even if apparently it isn’t to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular flaw or imperfection that you think is the most bothersome to you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think the hands are probably the most bothersome, if I had to pick one. The face thing bothers me too, especially in those newsletter photos where I start thinking it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it. But the hands are what I notice in the moment, right while I’m doing something.\n\nSince I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be up there pointing things out, and it’s like my attention just goes straight to my hands. I see the wrinkles, or I think one hand looks different than the other, or the fingers look uneven somehow, and then I’m not fully thinking about what I’m saying anymore. It feels so silly to say out loud, because they’re just hands, but that’s the thing I can’t seem to stop noticing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your appearance are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “a lot” is hard for me to judge, because I’m still doing the things I need to do. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but enough that it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might change over time, or do you feel like they're always going to be the same?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. But then other times, especially if I’m about to be in front of people or I know there might be a photo, it feels like the same worries come right back, like they’ve just been sitting there waiting.\n\nI don’t know if the actual thing I’m worried about would change, either. Right now it’s mostly my hands and my face in pictures, but I can’t tell if that means it’ll always be those exact things or if I’m just noticing them more because of what I’m doing at work lately. I guess I don’t feel certain either way. I hope it won’t always feel the same, but right now it’s hard for me to trust that it’ll just go away on its own.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific feature or detail about your hands or face that you think is the problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… with my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and this feeling that they look uneven. Like when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings, I’ll notice the skin across my knuckles or how the fingers look, and then I start thinking one hand looks different from the other. I don’t even know if that makes sense, because I know hands aren’t supposed to be perfectly identical, but once I see it, it kind of takes over my attention.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s more in photos than when I’m just looking in the mirror. In the newsletter pictures, I’ll look at myself and think my face looks crooked somehow, like one side isn’t sitting right or it’s not balanced. Other people have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it when I look at the picture. It’s embarrassing, because I know I probably sound like I’m picking myself apart, but those are the details I keep getting stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided any social or work-related activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I haven’t completely avoided work things, no. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. I wish I could say I know it will change, or that it’s just a phase and I’ll stop noticing it so much, but I don’t honestly know.\n\nSome days it feels like maybe it could ease up, because there are times when I’m busy enough or focused enough that I don’t think about it as much. But then other times, especially if I’m about to be in front of people or I know there might be a photo, it feels like the same worries come right back, like they’ve just been sitting there waiting.\n\nI don’t know if the actual thing I’m worried about would change, either. I guess I don’t feel certain either way. I hope it won’t always feel the same, but right now it’s hard for me to trust that it’ll just go away on its own.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific feature or detail about your hands or face that you think is the problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… with my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and this feeling that they look uneven. Like when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings, I’ll notice the skin across my knuckles or how the fingers look, and then I start thinking one hand looks different from the other. I don’t even know if that makes sense, because I know hands aren’t supposed to be perfectly identical, but once I see it, it kind of takes over my attention.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s more in photos than when I’m just looking in the mirror. In the newsletter pictures, I’ll look at myself and think my face looks crooked somehow, like one side isn’t sitting right or it’s not balanced. Other people have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it when I look at the picture. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it could, or at least I worry it could. It’s like… I didn’t set out to be bothered by these exact things. I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out. The wrinkles across the knuckles, the way one hand looks a little different from the other — I get stuck on it.

And then the face thing kind of came up more with the newsletter photos. I’ll see myself in a picture and think my face looks crooked or uneven, even when other people say they honestly don’t see what I’m talking about. So, yes, I guess if something else caught my attention the same way, maybe I could start focusing on that instead. It feels less like it’s only about hands or only about my face, and more like once I notice something, I have a hard time letting it go.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look, more than I think is normal. It started getting worse after I began helping with the safety trainings at work. I’m a production worker, and when I’m up there pointing things out to people, I can’t stop noticing my hands—like how wrinkly they look, and uneven, and I’ll be thinking about it while I’m supposed to be explaining something.\n\nAnd then it sort of carries over. If there’s a newsletter photo from work, I look at my face and it seems crooked to me, like one side just looks off. Other people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I keep seeing it. I know maybe it sounds small, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the way I look, yes. I mean, that’s what I came in for. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I’m up in front of people more, pointing things out, using my hands, and then I just get stuck looking at them. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven somehow, like one hand or the fingers don’t look right, and then I can’t get it out of my head.\n\nAnd then it isn’t only my hands, because if there’s a photo in the work newsletter, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, like one side is off. Other people say they don’t notice it, or they tell me it looks fine, but I still see it. So… no, not really something else exactly. It’s mainly that I can’t stop noticing those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it, honestly. When I’m helping with the safety trainings and I’m up there pointing at something, I start thinking people must be noticing my hands the way I do—how wrinkly they look, or uneven, like they don’t look right. And then I get distracted because I’m supposed to be explaining the safety steps, but in my head I’m thinking, “Are they looking at my hands? Do they think they look strange?”\n\nAnd with the newsletter photos, it’s similar. I’ll see my face and it looks crooked to me, like one side is off, and even if people say they don’t see it, I still wonder if they’re just being polite. So yes, I do worry about what people think, even though they tell me it looks fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything in a specific way, or repeat certain actions, to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I guess I didn’t think of it as repeating things at first. Before I have to be in front of people, like before one of the safety classes at work, or even if I’m going over to do a workshop at the senior center, I’ll end up in the bathroom checking the mirror more than once.\n\nI’ll look at my hands, put lotion on them, then look again to see if they seem less wrinkly or dry. And with my face, I’ll turn my head different ways in the mirror, like checking whether it still looks crooked from this angle or that angle. Sometimes I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll check one more time.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask a coworker something like, “Do I look okay?” or “Does my face look odd in this lighting?” I try to say it casually, but I’m really looking for reassurance. It does make me run a little behind sometimes, not terribly late, but enough that I’m rushing. I still make myself go, but I feel unsettled until I’ve done those things.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about other parts of your body besides your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no… I mean, I can get generally self-conscious, I guess, but the things that really grab my attention are my hands and my face.\n\nIt started feeling worse when I began helping with the safety trainings at work, because I’m up there pointing things out and using my hands, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow. And then with my face, it’s mostly when I see myself in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll think one side looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nSo, no, it’s not like I’m going from one body part to another all day. It’s mainly those two things that I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to worry about my hands now.” It just sort of jumps in, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing something out, trying to explain the steps, and suddenly all I can think is how wrinkly my hands look, or like they’re uneven somehow. I don’t want to be thinking about that, especially when I’m supposed to be focused on what I’m saying.\n\nAnd then with my face, it happens when I see those newsletter photos. I’ll just look at the picture and immediately think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s really how it feels—it comes into my mind before I can stop it, and then it’s hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to perform these checking behaviors, like looking in the mirror or asking for reassurance, in order to try to stop thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel kind of driven to do it. It’s not just, “Oh, I’ll glance in the mirror.” Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, and I’ll look at my hands, put lotion on, then look again like I’m trying to see if they look any better.\n\nAnd with my face, I’ll turn it different ways, almost like I’m checking from every angle. I know that sounds silly when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just leave it alone. If I don’t do it, the thought keeps nagging at me.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, too, trying to make it sound casual. It does calm me down a little, but then I hate that I asked. And it can make me run a little behind, so then I’m rushing to get to where I’m supposed to be. I still go, I don’t skip the class or the workshop, but I feel like I have to do those little checks first or I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and you'd rather not have them?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much unwanted. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m up there pointing things out. I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m saying, and instead my mind goes straight to how wrinkly they look, or like they’re uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then it’s the same kind of thing with my face in those newsletter photos from work. I’ll see one picture and suddenly I’m thinking, “My face looks crooked,” even though people tell me they don’t see it. I don’t want to be thinking about that. I’d rather just see the photo and move on, or do the training without noticing my hands the whole time. It feels like the thoughts grab onto me, and I really wish they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your appearance interfered with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a huge, life-stopping way, I guess. I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and volunteering. I haven’t stopped doing those things. But it does slow me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before a training, I’ll think I’m ready, and then I’ll end up checking how I look or looking at my hands again, and suddenly I’ve lost time. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be late or difficult, but I’m fussing around more than I used to.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve noticed I’ll hesitate more. If someone wants to take a picture, I’ll try to move out of the way or say, “Oh, no, you all go ahead,” like I’m joking, but really I just don’t want to see myself in it later. And sometimes before going to something I would normally enjoy, I’ll pause and worry about how I’m going to look, especially if there might be photos. I usually still go, but it takes more effort than it used to, and I don’t feel as relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hands or face that you think is flawed or imperfect?\n\nPatient: Yes. With my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and the way they look uneven to me, especially when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings. I’ll be standing there trying to explain something, and then I notice my fingers or the skin on my hands, and it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s that it looks crooked to me in the newsletter photos from work. I know other people have said they don’t see it, or they’ll say the picture looks fine, but when I look at it, that’s what jumps out at me. It feels very obvious to me, even if apparently it isn’t to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular flaw or imperfection that you think is the most bothersome to you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think the hands are probably the most bothersome, if I had to pick one. The face thing bothers me too, especially in those newsletter photos where I start thinking it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it. But the hands are what I notice in the moment, right while I’m doing something.\n\nSince I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be up there pointing things out, and it’s like my attention just goes straight to my hands. I see the wrinkles, or I think one hand looks different than the other, or the fingers look uneven somehow, and then I’m not fully thinking about what I’m saying anymore. It feels so silly to say out loud, because they’re just hands, but that’s the thing I can’t seem to stop noticing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your appearance are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “a lot” is hard for me to judge, because I’m still doing the things I need to do. I go to work, I still volunteer, I still help with the trainings, so it’s not like I’m staying home or completely falling apart over it.\n\nBut it does distress me more than I’d like to admit. It gets under my skin. Before trainings, especially, I can lose time getting ready or checking myself, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m moving slower than I should be. And with pictures, I’ve started ducking out of them or making excuses, like I’m just being polite or joking, but really I don’t want to see myself later and feel upset about it.\n\nAnd sometimes before social things I used to just go to without thinking, I hesitate now. I still usually go, but there’s this extra worry there that didn’t used to be so loud. So… yes, it bothers me. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but enough that it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might change over time, or do you feel like they're always going to be the same?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could say I know it will change, or that it’s just a phase and I’ll stop noticing it so much, but I don’t honestly know.\n\nSome days it feels like maybe it could ease up, because there are times when I’m busy enough or focused enough that I don’t think about it as much. But then other times, especially if I’m about to be in front of people or I know there might be a photo, it feels like the same worries come right back, like they’ve just been sitting there waiting.\n\nI don’t know if the actual thing I’m worried about would change, either. Right now it’s mostly my hands and my face in pictures, but I can’t tell if that means it’ll always be those exact things or if I’m just noticing them more because of what I’m doing at work lately. I guess I don’t feel certain either way. I hope it won’t always feel the same, but right now it’s hard for me to trust that it’ll just go away on its own.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific feature or detail about your hands or face that you think is the problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… with my hands, it’s mostly the wrinkles and this feeling that they look uneven. Like when I’m pointing at something during the safety trainings, I’ll notice the skin across my knuckles or how the fingers look, and then I start thinking one hand looks different from the other. I don’t even know if that makes sense, because I know hands aren’t supposed to be perfectly identical, but once I see it, it kind of takes over my attention.\n\nAnd with my face, it’s more in photos than when I’m just looking in the mirror. In the newsletter pictures, I’ll look at myself and think my face looks crooked somehow, like one side isn’t sitting right or it’s not balanced. Other people have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it when I look at the picture. It’s embarrassing, because I know I probably sound like I’m picking myself apart, but those are the details I keep getting stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided any social or work-related activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I haven’t completely avoided work things, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still volunteer, and if there’s a safety training I’m supposed to help with, I do show up. So in that sense it hasn’t stopped me from doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it has changed how I do it, if that makes sense. Before trainings, I can get slowed down because I’m trying to make sure I look okay, especially if I know people will be watching me demonstrate something or if my hands are going to be visible. I’ll fuss around more than I need to, and then I’m frustrated with myself because I’m taking longer and I know it shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nAnd with photos, yes, I’ve started avoiding those more. Like if someone wants a group picture for a newsletter or something at volunteering, I’ll kind of step back or offer to take the picture instead, or make some excuse like, “Oh, you all go ahead.” I don’t make a big scene about it, but I do try to get out of it.\n\nSocial things too… I usually still go, but I hesitate more now. Things I used to just say yes to without thinking, I’ll pause and wonder if I really want to be seen, or if there might be pictures. So I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped my whole life over it, but yes, I’ve been avoiding some parts of things, especially photos, and I’m slower and more anxious getting ready for things than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance could ever change to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: I suppose it could, yes. I mean, I didn’t come in thinking, “Oh, this is going to be about my hands and my face forever,” exactly. It’s just that since I started helping with the safety trainings, my hands are right there in front of me all the time, pointing things out, showing people what to do, and then I can’t stop noticing how wrinkly they look or how uneven they seem from one hand to the other.\n\nAnd then with the newsletter photos, that’s where my face started bothering me more, because I’ll see a picture and think it looks crooked, like one side is off somehow, even if other people say they don’t see anything. So I guess if the situation changed, maybe I’d latch onto something else. I don’t know for sure, but it does feel like once I start noticing a detail, it’s hard to unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the specific things that bother you about your appearance, like the wrinkles on your hands or the perceived crookedness of your face, could ever change to something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it could, or at least I worry it could. It’s like… I didn’t set out to be bothered by these exact things. I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out. The wrinkles across the knuckles, the way one hand looks a little different from the other — I get stuck on it.\n\nAnd then the face thing kind of came up more with the newsletter photos. I’ll see myself in a picture and think my face looks crooked or uneven, even when other people say they honestly don’t see what I’m talking about. So, yes, I guess if something else caught my attention the same way, maybe I could start focusing on that instead. It feels less like it’s only about hands or only about my face, and more like once I notice something, I have a hard time letting it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
